# 3. Sūrat Āl ʿImrān

**200 verses. Medinan.** It takes its name from a construct that occurs in verse 33: **وَءَالَ عِمْرَٰنَ** — *wa āla ʿImrān*, "**and the family of ʿImrān**". The construct occurs only once in the sūra; but from the place where it occurs the sūra stays inside that family for thirty verses: the wife of ʿImrān, Mary (Maryam), Zechariah (Zakariyyā), John (Yaḥyā), Jesus (ʿĪsā).

## This sūra is the continuation of al-Baqara

This has to be recorded at the outset, because it settles what is not repeated as one reads the sūra.

**The table below is not interpretation but an inventory of facts verifiable from the two texts:**

| Fact | al-Baqara | Āl ʿImrān |
|---|---|---|
| Opening letters | *Alif-lām-mīm* (2/1) | *Alif-lām-mīm* (3/1) |
| **ٱللَّهُ لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا هُوَ ٱلْحَىُّ ٱلْقَيُّومُ** | 2/255 — in the **middle** of the sūra | 3/2 — at the **head** of the sūra |
| Qibla, pilgrimage, Kaʿba | 2/125-129, 2/142-150 | 3/96-97 — **short, in the form of a reminder** |
| Debate with the People of the Book | 2/40-141 — **a long block** | 3/64-99 — **the second block** |
| The identity of Abraham | 2/130-141 | 3/65-68 |
| The prohibition of *ribā* | 2/275-281 — **detailed** | 3/130 — **a single verse, a reminder** |
| Closing | *Rabbanā lā tuʾākhidhnā…* (2/286) — **a prayer** | *Rabbanā… fa'fu ʿannā* (3/193-194) — **a prayer** |
| Final command | *Fa'nṣurnā ʿala'l-qawmi'l-kāfirīn* | *Wa'ttaqu'llāha laʿallakum tufliḥūn* (200) |

**In the classical sources these two sūras are named together and called *al-Zahrāwān* — "the two radiant sūras".** I record that this naming is widespread; I do not ascribe it word for word to any particular report.

**And this has a concrete consequence in this commentary:** the roots, concepts and narratives already analysed in al-Baqara **will not be told again** here. *Taqwā*, *ribā*, *infāq*, *al-birr*, the qibla, *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿa*, *kun fa-yakūn*, *al-ʿurwa al-wuthqā*, *ummatan wasaṭan* — all were treated in `002-bakara.md`. Here only **what Āl ʿImrān adds to them** will be recorded.

**The same holds for the passage on Mary and Jesus:** in `019-meryem.md` the prayer of Zechariah, Mary's withdrawal, the annunciation and the birth were treated in detail. **The work to be done here is to show the *difference* between the two tellings** — because the telling of the same account in two separate sūras in two separate ways is a datum belonging to the text itself.

---

## The axis of the sūra: how a community is to build itself

The subject of the sūra cannot be said in a single word; but it can be shown from the text that it gathers around three questions:

1. **How is the text to be read?** — verse 7: *muḥkam* and *mutashābih*.
2. **How is one to speak with the other?** — verse 64: *kalimatin sawāʾin baynanā wa baynakum*.
3. **How is defeat to be read?** — 121-179: the Uḥud block.

**And what the three have in common is worth recording — I give this as my own reading:** in three separate fields the sūra takes aim at **the same error**. In reading the text, a person's making the text say his own inclination (7); in speaking with the other side, his failing to see what is shared (64); after a defeat, his looking for the cause outside himself (152-153, 165). **In all three the problem is that a person does not see his own share.**

The sūra hands that share back three times, in three separate places, explicitly:

| Verse | Sentence | To whom |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *Fa-ammā'lladhīna fī qulūbihim **zayghun*** | **To the reader** — the inclination is in the one reading |
| **152** | *Ḥattā idhā **fashiltum wa tanāzaʿtum** fi'l-amri **wa ʿaṣaytum*** | **To the community** — three verbs, all three theirs |
| **165** | *Qul **huwa min ʿindi anfusikum*** | **Directly** — "it is from yourselves" |

**Three sentences doing the same work in three separate fields.** This is a weave that can be verified from the text.

---

## The structure of the sūra

The sūra can be divided into seven blocks. The block boundaries are marked either by a change of addressee or by a form of address (*yā ahla'l-kitāb*, *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū*).

| Block | Verses | Subject | What closes the block |
|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | 1-32 | The sending down of the Book, **muḥkam and mutashābih**, the ornament of this world, the owner of dominion, the limit of friendship | *Inna'llāha lā yuḥibbu'l-kāfirīn* (32) |
| **II** | 33-63 | **The chosen line:** the family of ʿImrān, Mary, Zechariah, John, Jesus, the mutual imprecation | *Wa inna'llāha la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'l-ḥakīm* (62) |
| **III** | 64-99 | **Address to the People of the Book:** the common word, the identity of Abraham, trust, the covenant of the prophets | *Wa'llāhu shahīdun ʿalā mā taʿmalūn* (98) |
| **IV** | 100-120 | **The inside of the community:** *iʿtiṣām*, a community calling to the good, *khayr umma*, *laysū sawāʾ* | *Inna'llāha bimā yaʿmalūna muḥīṭ* (120) |
| **V** | 121-179 | **The Uḥud block:** the setting out at dawn, the faltering, the limit of responsibility, consultation, the slain | *Wa lakum ajrun ʿaẓīm* (179) |
| **VI** | 180-189 | Miserliness, the words "God is poor", the pledge to make the Book plain, testing | *Wa lahum ʿadhābun alīm* (188) |
| **VII** | 190-200 | **Ulu'l-albāb**, the long prayer, the closing commands | *Wa'ttaqu'llāha laʿallakum tufliḥūn* (200) |

### Repetitions within the sūra — countable data

**The table below is not interpretation but an inventory of repetitions verifiable from the text.**

| Root / word | Where | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **ك-ت-ب** (book) | 3, 7, 19, 20, 23, 48, 64, 65, 69, 70, 71, 72, 75, 78, 79, 81, 98, 99, 100, 110, 113, 119, 145, 184, 186, 187, 199 | **The densest root in the sūra** |
| **أهل الكتاب** | 64, 65, 69, 70, 71, 72, 75, 98, 99, 110, 113, 199 | Twelve occurrences; **two of them with a distinguishing caveat** (113, 199) |
| **ص-ف-و** (*iṣṭifāʾ*) | *iṣṭafā* (33 — twice), *iṣṭafāki* (42 — twice) | **The verb of choosing, four times, in two verses** |
| **ن-ذ-ر** | *nadhartu* (35) | The vow — the opening verb of the account |
| **م-ر-ي-م** | 36, 37, 42, 43, 44, 45 | Mary's name six times |
| **ع-ي-س-ى** | 45, 52, 55, 59, 84 | Jesus's name five times |
| **ط-و-ع / ع-ص-ي** | *aṭīʿu'llāha wa'r-rasūl* (32, 132) — *ʿaṣaytum* (152) | **Command, command, breach** |
| **ن-ص-ر** | 13, 52, 56, 81, 111, 123, 126, 147, 150, 160 | The root of help — **most of them in the Uḥud block** |
| **ص-ب-ر** | 17, 120, 125, 142, 146, 186, 200 | **It returns at the end as a command** |
| **و-ه-ن** | *lā tahinū* (139) — *mā wahanū* (146) | **The prohibition and the example** |
| **ف-ر-ق** | *lā tafarraqū* (103) — *tafarraqū* (105) | **The ban and the breach** |
| **ب-ي-ن-ة / ب-ي-ن** | *mā jāʾahumu'l-bayyināt* (105) — *al-bayyināt* (183, 184) | The coming of the evidence |
| **أ-م-ر ب-ال-م-ع-ر-و-ف** | 104, 110, 114 | **Three times; all three in the same block** |
| **ك-ظ-م** | *al-kāẓimīna'l-ghayẓ* (134) | The only occurrence of this form in the Qurʾān |
| **ش-و-ر** | *wa shāwirhum fi'l-amr* (159) | **The only occurrence of this verb in this shape in the Qurʾān** |
| **س-و-ي / سَوَآء** | *kalimatin **sawāʾin*** (64) — *laysū **sawāʾun*** (113) | **The same word: common ground, and "they are not all alike"** |
| **ل-ب-ب** | *ūli'l-albāb* (7, 190) | **At the two ends of the sūra** |
| **ر-ب-ب (prayer)** | *rabbanā* / *rabbi* — 8, 9, 16, 35, 38, 40, 41, 47, 53, 147, 191, 192, 193, 194 | **Fourteen calls** |
| **ح-س-ب** | *lā taḥsabanna* / *lā yaḥsabanna* — 169, 178, 180, 188 | **Four times: "let them not suppose"** |
| **م-ل-ك** | *mālika'l-mulk… al-mulk* (26 — four times) | Four occurrences in a single verse |
| **و-ل-ج** | *tūliju* (27 — twice) | *Īlāj* — night and day |

### The verse-ending

Looking at the ends of the two hundred verses, **there is no single dominant sound pattern.** The sūra moves in long and composite sentences; the verse-ends fall for the most part on **the names of God** or on **plural verbs**: *al-ḥakīm, al-akhyār, al-mīʿād, ḥisāb, ʿalīm, baṣīr, shahīd, khabīr, taʿmalūn, tufliḥūn.*

**What is to be recorded is this, and it is a verifiable fact:** verses ending in **pairs of names** are dense throughout the sūra (*al-ʿazīzu'l-ḥakīm*, *samīʿun ʿalīm*, *ghafūrun raḥīm*, *raʾūfun bi'l-ʿibād*), and these pairs are most often chosen to suit the subject of the verse. This is a feature shared by the long-versed Medinan sūras, and it was recorded in `002-bakara.md`.

**No claim about numbers or reckoning is being made.**

### The revelation and its historical place

That the sūra is Medinan is widely agreed among the sources. **Two blocks within it rest plainly on a historical ground:**

- **Verses 121-179** speak of a battle and of the defeat that followed it. In the classical sources this is identified as **Uḥud**, and the text itself also refers back to an earlier one (in verse 13, *fiʾatayni'ltaqatā*).
- **Verses 33-63** speak of a debate with the People of the Book that runs in particular through the position of Jesus; **verse 61 (the mutual imprecation)** is the most intense point of that debate.

**Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I draw a boundary here, and I will keep it throughout the sūra:**

**One.** In the discussions of Uḥud and of the mutual imprecation **I do not go into the detail of the reports or into personal names.** The verses themselves name no one anywhere — no commander, no tribe, no member of a delegation. **This silence of the text will be preserved as a choice.**

**Two.** The sūra contains both severe and laudatory statements about the People of the Book. **Verse 113 (*laysū sawāʾ* — "they are not all alike") is the verse that places all these statements on record**, and in this commentary every statement of the sūra concerning the People of the Book will be read together with that caveat. This is not a sensitivity brought in from outside; **it is the caveat the sūra itself sets down.**

**Three.** **No blanket ruling will be issued about any religious or ethnic group.** What the verses describe is **attributes**; whoever carries the attribute is the one the verse speaks of.

**Four.** **No legal ruling will be issued; no entry will be made into present-day politics.**

The basmala was treated in earlier sections; the verses are numbered without it.

---

## 3/1-2 — الٓمٓ · ٱللَّهُ لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا هُوَ ٱلْحَىُّ ٱلْقَيُّومُ

*Alif-lām-mīm · Allāhu lā ilāha illā huwa'l-ḥayyu'l-qayyūm*

"Alif-lām-mīm. God — there is no god but He; **al-Ḥayy**, **al-Qayyūm**."

**The note on the disconnected letters (*al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿa*) was given in detail at 2/1 in `002-bakara.md`:** there is no certain knowledge; the views were tabulated there and no preference was imposed. **I do not repeat it here.**

**And the construct *al-Ḥayyu'l-Qayyūm* was analysed at 2/255 in `002-bakara.md` (the Verse of the Throne).** In summary: *ḥayy* — living; *qayyūm* is an intensive form not from ك-و-م but from the root **ق-و-م**: **the one who stands of Himself and holds up everything else.**

**Here I add what belongs to Āl ʿImrān alone — it is a difference of position, and it is verifiable:**

| Sūra | Where the sentence stands | What comes before it |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Baqara 2/255** | The **middle** of the sūra | After a long block of rulings and narrative |
| **Āl ʿImrān 3/2** | The **second verse** of the sūra | Only the opening letters |

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** in al-Baqara this sentence is a **stopping place** inside a long narration; in Āl ʿImrān it is **the ground.** Everything the sūra will speak of across two hundred verses — book, family, war, defeat — it builds on top of this sentence. And the third verse, immediately after this name, will name **the Book**.

---

## 3/3-4 — نَزَّلَ عَلَيْكَ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ بِٱلْحَقِّ مُصَدِّقًا لِّمَا بَيْنَ يَدَيْهِ وَأَنزَلَ ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةَ وَٱلْإِنجِيلَ · مِن قَبْلُ هُدًى لِّلنَّاسِ وَأَنزَلَ ٱلْفُرْقَانَ

*Nazzala ʿalayka'l-kitāba bi'l-ḥaqqi muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayhi wa anzala'l-Tawrāta wa'l-Injīl · Min qablu hudan li'n-nāsi wa anzala'l-furqān*

"He has sent down the Book to you, **confirming what was before it**, with the truth. **And the Torah and the Gospel too** — before this, as guidance for people — He sent down. And He sent down **the furqān**."

### Two verbs: نَزَّلَ and أَنزَلَ

**Two different forms of the same root (ن-ز-ل), in the same sentence, for two different objects.**

| Verb | Form | Its object |
|---|---|---|
| **نَزَّلَ** | Form II (*tafʿīl*) | **al-Kitāb** — the Qurʾān |
| **أَنزَلَ** | Form IV (*ifʿāl*) | **al-Tawrāt and al-Injīl**, then **al-Furqān** |

**The distinction recorded in the classical sources is this:** Form II conveys a **gradual** descent (piece by piece, at intervals), Form IV a descent **all at once**.

**This distinction was treated at 47/20 in `047-muhammed.md`, and this caveat was entered there: the distinction is transmitted in the classical sources; but it has also been pointed out that it is not applied consistently everywhere in the Qurʾān.** I rely on that treatment and keep the same caveat.

**The usage here agrees with that distinction, and this is a verifiable fact:** the Qurʾān was spread over twenty-three years; the Torah and the Gospel are named in the verse in the past tense, as completed. **But I do not establish this as a rule.**

### مُصَدِّقًا لِّمَا بَيْنَ يَدَيْهِ

The root **ص-د-ق**: to be true. **The active participle of Form II: *muṣaddiq* — the one that confirms, that verifies.**

**And the construction must be recorded: *limā bayna yadayh* — "what is between his two hands".** In Arabic this idiom conveys **what stands in front, what is at hand**. The sense "the earlier books" therefore comes from the idiom.

**I record this as an observation:** the verse places the newly come text not **against** the earlier one but **on top of** it. **And this is the ground of the call to common ground that will be made in verse 64 of the sūra** — because the word to be spoken there lies along the same line.

### ٱلْفُرْقَان

The root **ف-ر-ق**: to separate. ***Furqān* is that which separates.**

**The word was treated in detail at 8/29 in `008-enfal.md` and in `025-furkan.md`.** I do not repeat it here; in summary: the word is used both for **a text** and for **a capacity to distinguish**.

**And there is a question about the occurrence here, and the classical commentaries differ over it:**

| Reading | What *al-Furqān* is | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **The Qurʾān itself** — named by a second name | The Qurʾān uses this name of itself elsewhere (al-Furqān 25/1) |
| 2 | **A quality shared by all revelation** — every one of them separates | The verb *wa anzala* being repeated a third time |
| 3 | **The capacity to tell truth from falsehood** | At al-Anfāl 8/29 the word occurs not as a text but as something given |

**All three readings are transmitted. I do not impose a preference.**

**وَٱللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ ذُو ٱنتِقَامٍ** — and the verse closes the matter of the Book with **a pair of names**. The word *intiqām* is used in the Qurʾān of God alone, and it means not **personal anger** but **the exacting of what is due**; the root **ن-ق-م** was treated in the series at 44/16 in `044-duhan.md`.

---

## 3/5-6 — إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَا يَخْفَىٰ عَلَيْهِ شَىْءٌ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَلَا فِى ٱلسَّمَآءِ · هُوَ ٱلَّذِى يُصَوِّرُكُمْ فِى ٱلْأَرْحَامِ كَيْفَ يَشَآءُ

*Inna'llāha lā yakhfā ʿalayhi shayʾun fi'l-arḍi wa lā fi's-samāʾ · Huwa'lladhī yuṣawwirukum fi'l-arḥāmi kayfa yashāʾ*

"Truly from God **nothing on earth or in heaven is hidden.** · **He it is who shapes you in the wombs as He wills.**"

### The order: earth first, heaven after

**The Qurʾān's usual order is *al-samāwāt wa'l-arḍ* — heaven first.** Here it is reversed: ***fi'l-arḍi wa lā fi's-samāʾ.***

**I record this as an observation about the word order, and I build no firm ruling on it.** In the classical commentaries it is said that this is connected with the following verse: the sixth verse speaks of **the womb** — that is, of the most hidden place on earth.

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the order appears to be built on the **degree** of hiddenness. What is in heaven is far but visible; what is on earth is near but **covered**. And the verse puts the covered one first.

### يُصَوِّرُكُمْ فِى ٱلْأَرْحَامِ

The root **ص-و-ر**: **to give a thing its form, to draw a shape.** *Ṣūra* — form. Form II (*taṣwīr*) conveys repetition and fineness of work.

**The root was treated at 59/24 in `059-hasr.md` (*al-Bāriʾu'l-Muṣawwir*) and at 64/3 in `064-tegabun.md`.** I do not repeat it here.

**The caveat here is worth recording: *kayfa yashāʾ* — "as He wills".** **That is, the verse takes as its subject not the *source* of the form but the *disposal* of it.**

**And its position matters — I give this as my own reading:** at the thirty-fifth verse the sūra will enter an account that opens with a woman vowing **what is in her womb**, and that woman will give birth to **other than what she expected**. **The sixth verse thus lays down the ruling of that account forty verses in advance:** the form in the womb is not in the hands of the one who wants it.

**I record explicitly that this is not being read as a "scientific sign".** The verse is not describing embryology; it is saying **in whose hands the disposal lies.**

---

## 3/7 — هُوَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَنزَلَ عَلَيْكَ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ مِنْهُ ءَايَٰتٌ مُّحْكَمَٰتٌ هُنَّ أُمُّ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ وَأُخَرُ مُتَشَٰبِهَٰتٌ

*Huwa'lladhī anzala ʿalayka'l-kitāba minhu āyātun muḥkamātun hunna ummu'l-kitābi wa ukharu mutashābihāt; fa-ammā'lladhīna fī qulūbihim zayghun fa-yattabiʿūna mā tashābaha minhu'btighāʾa'l-fitnati wa'btighāʾa taʾwīlih; wa mā yaʿlamu taʾwīlahu illa'llāh; wa'r-rāsikhūna fi'l-ʿilmi yaqūlūna āmannā bihī kullun min ʿindi rabbinā; wa mā yadhdhakkaru illā ūlu'l-albāb*

"**He it is who sent down the Book to you.** Part of it is **muḥkam** verses — **they are the mother of the Book**; the others are **mutashābih**. Those in whose hearts is **a crookedness** pursue what is *mutashābih* in it, **seeking discord** and **seeking its interpretation**. No one but God knows its interpretation. **Those firmly rooted in knowledge** say: 'We believe in it; all of it is from our Lord.' And none but **those who possess the kernel** takes heed."

---

**This verse is one of the most important verses of this commentary in point of method.** For here the text speaks about **how it is itself to be read**. And what it says is not a method but a **diagnosis**: the same text does two different pieces of work in two different hearts.

I shall analyse the verse under six headings: **muḥkam**, **mutashābih**, *ummu'l-kitāb*, ***zaygh***, ***taʾwīl***, and last **the question of the pause** — that is, where the sentence stops.

### مُحْكَمَٰت — root: ح-ك-م

**The concrete sense of the root has been recorded many times in the series** (`044-duhan.md`, `051-zariyat.md`, `022-hac.md`, `002-bakara.md` 2/269): **ح-ك-م — to prevent, to rein in, to hold in place.** And the picture of the root is a tool: **حَكَمَة (ḥakama)** — the **bit** put in a horse's mouth. What keeps the animal from swerving.

**Hence *muḥkam*: what has no slack, what cannot be moved, what cannot be slid about.**

**At 47/20 in `047-muhammed.md` the same word was treated as the attribute of a sūra** (*sūratun muḥkamatun*), and this was recorded there:

> *"An ambiguous word allows everyone to interpret it as he pleases; a muḥkam word does not."*

**I rely on that treatment.**

**And at 11/1 in `011-hud.md` the same root occurred as a verb:** *kitābun **uḥkimat** āyātuhū thumma fuṣṣilat* — "a Book whose verses have been **made firm**, then set out in detail." What was recorded there can be carried over here: **iḥkām is the attribute of the Book *as a whole*; tafṣīl is its being *reachable*.**

**And this at once raises a question: if Hūd 11/1 says *uḥkimat* of the whole Book, why does Āl ʿImrān 3/7 divide the Book in two?** I shall take this question up shortly in a table — because the same question arises for *mutashābih* as well, and there it is sharper.

### مُتَشَٰبِهَٰت — root: ش-ب-ه

The root **ش-ب-ه**: **to resemble.** *Shibh* — a likeness. **Form VI (*tafāʿul*): *tashābaha* — to resemble one another, to resemble so closely as to be confused.**

**The idea at the centre of the root is not "obscurity" but *indistinguishability*.** Two things resemble each other so closely that which is which cannot be picked out.

**And this can be verified from another use of the word in the Qurʾān:** at al-Baqara 2/70, in the account of the cow, *inna'l-baqara **tashābaha** ʿalaynā* — "the cattle **seem alike** to us", that is, we cannot tell them apart.

**Now the real matter.** ***Mutashābih* is used in the Qurʾān of the *whole* of the Book as well**, and this was treated in this commentary.

**At 39/23 in `039-zumer.md` this was recorded:**

> *"**مُتَشَابِه** — root **ش-ب-ه**: to resemble. Its use here differs from the distinction *muḥkam / mutashābih*: there the verses whose meaning is closed were meant; here **the whole of the Book** is named with this adjective. The lexicographers give the sense here as 'its parts holding together, consistent in style and in content'."*

**I rely on that treatment and separate the two uses in a table. This table is the most important table of this section:**

| | **al-Zumar 39/23** | **Āl ʿImrān 3/7** |
|---|---|---|
| Wording | *kitāban **mutashābihan** mathāniya* | *wa ukharu **mutashābihāt*** |
| **Extent** | **The whole of the Book** | **A part of the Book** |
| **Is there a counterpart** | **No** — there is no division | **Yes** — *muḥkamāt* |
| **Sense** | Its parts **holding together**, its style and content **consistent** | Its sense **not determinable on its own**, open to more than one possibility |
| What resembles what | **Its own parts resemble one another** | **Two senses resemble each other** — they cannot be told apart |
| Evaluative weight | **Praise** — a mark of excellence | **Neutral** — a condition of the text |
| Its outcome | A shiver and then a softening (*taqshaʿirru… thumma talīnu*) | **It divides in two according to the reader** |

**And the conclusion that follows from the table must be recorded, because it heads off a frequent error:** it cannot be said that "the Qurʾān speaks inconsistently about itself" merely because the same word occurs in two places. **The sense of the root is the same in both — *resemblance*.** What changes is **what resembles what**: in al-Zumar the parts resemble one another (harmony); in Āl ʿImrān the senses resemble one another (indistinguishability).

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the same root describes in one place the **inner consistency** of the text and in the other the reader's **inability to decide**. **And the two do not conflict, because the two look at two different faces of the text** — one at the text itself, the other at the distance between the text and the reader.

### On what basis are muḥkam and mutashābih separated — the classical explanations

**In the classical commentaries the criterion of this distinction is disputed. I tabulate the principal explanations and impose no preference.**

| # | Criterion | What *muḥkam* is | What *mutashābih* is |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Intelligibility** | What its wording bears one sense | What its wording leaves open to more than one sense |
| 2 | **Knowability** | What the intellect can reach | What is known only by report (the hour of the resurrection, the spirit, the detail of the unseen) |
| 3 | **Actionability** | What can be acted upon — the rulings | What is not tied directly to any act |
| 4 | **The field of transcendence** | The plain statements about God | Statements ascribing human acts and limbs to God (*yad*, *istiwāʾ*, *wajh*) |
| 5 | **The disconnected letters** | All the other verses | The cut letters at the heads of sūras |
| 6 | **Abrogation** | What remains in force | What has had its ruling lifted |

**All these explanations are transmitted in the classical sources.** Some of them do not exclude one another; there are also those who say that more than one criterion operates at once.

**And it must be recorded with which criterion this distinction has been worked in the series — this is a statement of method:** in this commentary the term *mutashābih* has been used consistently in the **fourth sense** (statements ascribing human acts to God) and in the **fifth sense** (the disconnected letters). **Examples:** `089-fecr.md` 89/22 (*wa jāʾa rabbuka*), `057-hadid.md` 57/29 (*bi-yadi'llāh*), `069-hakka.md` 69/17 (the bearing of the throne), `036-yasin.md` (*aydīnā*), `050-kaf.md` and `002-bakara.md` (the disconnected letters). **And in all those places the same stance was kept:** the two attitudes (*tafwīḍ* and *taʾwīl*) were tabulated and no preference was imposed.

**That is, this verse names a piece of work the series has already been doing.**

### هُنَّ أُمُّ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ — "the mother of the Book"

The root **أ-م-م**: **origin, source, that to which one returns.** *Umm* — mother; *imām* — the one in front; *umma* — those gathered around one origin.

**The root was treated at 2/78 in `002-bakara.md` and at 42/7 in `042-sura.md` (*umm al-qurā*).** I do not repeat it here.

**And a point of word order must be recorded, and the classical commentaries have dwelt on it:** the subject is plural (*hunna* — "they"), the predicate is **singular** (*umm* — "mother"). It does not say "they are the mothers" (*ummahātu'l-kitāb*).

**The classical explanation is this, and I transmit it:** *umm* is used here as a **generic term** — the plurality goes back to a single origin. That is, the *muḥkam* verses do not form **separate origins but a single origin**.

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the singular predicate implies that the *muḥkam* verses are **a single body that does not contradict itself**. And the root sense of the word *umm* completes this: **the place to which one returns.** That is, what is *mutashābih* is read by being **returned** to what is *muḥkam* — not the reverse.

**This is the only note of method the verse gives, and it comes out of the verse's own wording.**

### زَيْغ — root: ز-ي-غ

**And now the diagnostic part of the verse.**

**The root was analysed in detail at 53/17 in `053-necm.md` (*mā zāgha'l-baṣar*) and at 61/5 in `061-saff.md`.** I take the description given there:

> *"Its sense: **to deviate from a straight line, to incline, to bend.** *Zāghati'l-shams* — the sun declined from its zenith. **At the centre of the root stands this: a departure from a line, a difference of angle.** And this is the most important feature of the word: ***zaygh* is not a sudden break but an *inclination*.** The sun does not drop from its zenith all at once; it parts from it by a small angle, and that angle grows with time."*

**I rely on that treatment and add here what belongs to Āl ʿImrān alone.**

**At al-Najm 53/17 the *zaygh* is in the eye; here it is in the heart.**

| Place | Where | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Najm 53/17** | *mā zāgha'l-**baṣar*** | The **eye** did not swerve — the soundness of the witnessing |
| **Āl ʿImrān 3/7** | *fī **qulūbihim** zaygh* | A crookedness **in the heart** — the corruption of the reading |
| **Āl ʿImrān 3/8** | *rabbanā lā **tuzigh** qulūbanā* | The same root, as a **prayer** |

**Three occurrences, one root, three separate positions.** This is a fact verifiable from the text.

**And the word order must be recorded: *fī qulūbihim zayghun* — "in their hearts is a crookedness".** The sentence is a nominal sentence, and *zaygh* comes **indefinite**. **That is, the verse does not say "their hearts are crooked"; it says "in their hearts is a crookedness".** The difference is not small: the first defines the person, the second records **a condition**.

**I give this as my own reading, and its ground is the indefinite form:** the verse is not describing a **group**, it is describing a **state**. And that state will be the subject of the believers' prayer in the eighth verse — that is, the verse says indirectly that the state is closed to no one.

### ٱبْتِغَآءَ ٱلْفِتْنَةِ

The root **ب-غ-ي**: **to want, to seek; and to exceed the limit.** The verbal noun of Form VIII, *ibtighāʾ* — **to seek out, to go after.** The root was treated at 2/213 in `002-bakara.md`, at 42/14 in `042-sura.md` and at 45/17 in `045-casiye.md` (*baghyan baynahum*).

The root **ف-ت-ن**: **to put gold into the fire and refine it.** The root was treated at 29/2-3 in `029-ankebut.md` and at 8/25 in `008-enfal.md`. Its sense here is given in two ways in the classical commentaries:

| Reading | What *fitna* is |
|---|---|
| 1 | **To stir up confusion** — to set people against one another |
| 2 | **To lead astray** — to gather proof-texts for one's own view and draw others to it |

**Both are transmitted; they do not exclude one another.**

**And what must be recorded is the verbal noun itself: *ibtighāʾ* — seeking.** **The verse is not describing a *coming upon* the *mutashābih* but a going after it.** The verb is *yattabiʿūna* — "**they follow**, they go after."

**I record this as my own reading:** what the verse condemns is not the reading of what is *mutashābih*; it is **leaving the *muḥkam* aside and choosing the *mutashābih* alone.** For *ittibāʿ* is a verb of turning towards — a person chooses what he will turn to. **And the reason for the choice is written plainly in the verse: the crookedness in the heart.** The order is therefore this: first the inclination, then the choice, then the text.

**This coincides with a measure this commentary has recorded again and again:** at 2/213 in `002-bakara.md`, at 42/14 in `042-sura.md` and at 45/17 in `045-casiye.md` it was recorded that division arises **not out of ignorance but after knowledge**. **Here the same thing is said at the level of reading a text:** the problem is not the closedness of the text but the inclination that turns to what is closed.

### تَأْوِيل — root: أ-و-ل

**The root was analysed in `012-yusuf.md` as the key construct of that sūra (*taʾwīlu'l-aḥādīth*) and at 18/78-82 in `018-kehf.md` (*dhālika taʾwīlu mā lam tasṭiʿ ʿalayhi ṣabrā*).** I take the description given there:

> *"The root **أ-و-ل**: **to arrive at the origin of a thing, at the place it returns to.** *Āla ilayhi* — it returned to it, it arrived at it. *Awwal* — the first point, the one returned to. **From this comes *taʾwīl*: to carry a thing to where it arrives, to show its end.**"*

**And the use in al-Kahf gives the full picture of the word:** three events had occurred there — the holing of the boat, the killing of the boy, the setting straight of the wall — and Moses (Mūsā) had objected to all three. **The *taʾwīl* spoken of in the eighty-second verse was not the *meaning* of the events but *where they arrived*.** That is, *taʾwīl* is the **outcome** of what is seen; not an interpretation drawn from it.

**This is decisive for the verse before us, and it can be given in a table:**

| Word | What it is | Where it was treated |
|---|---|---|
| **تَفْسِير** | The explanation of the wording, the saying of what it says | — |
| **تَأْوِيل** | **Where the utterance arrives**, the state it comes to be | `012-yusuf.md`, `018-kehf.md` 18/82 |

**And from here we come to the most disputed place in the verse.** For if *taʾwīl* is understood as "interpretation" the sentence says one thing, and if it is understood as "the state arrived at" it says another.

### وَمَا يَعْلَمُ تَأْوِيلَهُۥٓ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ وَٱلرَّٰسِخُونَ فِى ٱلْعِلْمِ — the question of the pause

**This is one of the oldest and best-known questions of the pause (*waqf*) in Qurʾānic exegesis. Where the sentence stops changes directly what the verse says.**

**There are two readings, and both are transmitted in the classical sources with strong defenders. I give them in a table and I IMPOSE NO PREFERENCE.**

| | **Reading A — the stop is at *illa'llāh*** | **Reading B — the stop is at *fi'l-ʿilm*** |
|---|---|---|
| How the sentence is divided | *…wa mā yaʿlamu taʾwīlahu **illa'llāh.** · Wa'r-rāsikhūna fi'l-ʿilmi yaqūlūna āmannā bihī…* | *…wa mā yaʿlamu taʾwīlahu illa'llāhu **wa'r-rāsikhūna fi'l-ʿilm.** · Yaqūlūna āmannā bihī…* |
| Sense | **God alone** knows its interpretation. Those firmly rooted in knowledge say "we believe". | God **and those firmly rooted in knowledge** know its interpretation. They say "we believe". |
| What *wa'r-rāsikhūna* is | **The subject of a new sentence** (*mubtadaʾ*) | **In apposition to the word *Allāh*** (*maʿṭūf*) |
| What *yaqūlūna* is | **The predicate** (*khabar*) — the predicate of the sentence | **A circumstantial clause** (*ḥāl*) — "…saying, while saying" |
| Its ground | **One.** *Yaqūlūna*, coming after the particle *wa*, needs a predicate; on reading A that need is met naturally. **Two.** The verse as a whole draws a **limit**: going after the *mutashābih* is condemned. **Three.** The limit of knowledge is set down plainly elsewhere in the Qurʾān as well. | **One.** That being *firmly rooted in knowledge* is named in praise is felt to require that the rootedness have some **return**. **Two.** The word *rāsikh* is an attribute of **knowing**; to speak of not knowing immediately afterwards is felt to be weak. **Three.** In some early reports on the readings and in exegesis this reading has been preferred. |
| What *taʾwīl* is on this reading | Chiefly **the state that will come about** — the hour of the resurrection, the detail of the unseen | Chiefly **the resolution of the meaning** — the returning of the text to what is *muḥkam* |
| Its outcome | **Silence** is a virtue | **Going deeper** is a possibility |

**And the last two rows of the table explain why the disagreement has lasted so long — I record this as my own reading:** the two readings in fact give the word *taʾwīl* two different senses. **On reading A *taʾwīl* is "the state to be arrived at", and knowing that really is not in human hands.** **On reading B *taʾwīl* is "the resolution of the meaning", and that is something reachable by work.** That is, before the disagreement is about where the sentence stops, it is about which sense the word is taken in.

**I give this as an observation; I prefer neither of the two readings and impose neither on the reader.**

**And one further caveat must be entered, because the disagreement is often pulled to one side:**

**Both readings can be drawn from the text, and both have been defended in the classical tradition. To make weapons of them against each other comes close to the very thing the verse condemns — *ibtighāʾa'l-fitna*.** I write this as a note of method.

**And this too is a verifiable fact:** neither of the two readings forbids the **reading** of the *mutashābih* verses. The verse lays down no ban on reading; it makes a distinction of **intention**: between the one who approaches with *ibtighāʾa'l-fitna* and the one who says *āmannā bih*.

### ٱلرَّٰسِخُونَ فِى ٱلْعِلْمِ

The root **ر-س-خ**: **to strike root deep, to be fixed fast.** The lexicographers record that the word is used of **a mountain settling on the ground** and of **a tree striking root**.

**The picture of the root is worth recording:** *rusūkh* conveys not height but **depth**. **That is, the verse speaks not of those who have *risen* in knowledge but of those who have *struck root* in it.**

**And the preposition must be recorded: *rāsikhūna fi'l-ʿilm* — "inside knowledge".** Not *ʿalā* (on top of it) but *fī* (inside it).

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the word pictures knowledge not as a **height** but as a **ground**. And the function of a ground is not to topple when it is shaken. **The verse shows this at once:** the one with a crookedness in his heart, meeting a closed verse, is **thrown**; the one who has struck root, meeting the same verse, **stands** and says *"all of it is from our Lord"*.

### كُلٌّ مِّنْ عِندِ رَبِّنَا

**The sentence spoken is worth recording, because it does not *resolve* the *mutashābih*.**

*Kullun min ʿindi rabbinā* — "**all of it** is from our Lord." **That is, the sentence does not pass off what it has not understood as understood; it records that what it has not understood comes from the same source.**

**I record this as an observation:** the verse puts into the mouth of the one firmly rooted in knowledge not an **explanation** but a **placing**. **And the word *kull* covers the *muḥkam* as well as the *mutashābih*** — that is, the verse that divides the Book in two does not divide acceptance in two.

### وَمَا يَذَّكَّرُ إِلَّآ أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَلْبَٰبِ

The root **ل-ب-ب**. **It was treated at 39/18 in `039-zumer.md`, and I take the description given there:**

> *"*albāb* is the plural of *lubb*: **the kernel of a thing, its inside;** the edible part of a fruit, the part separated from its husk. **That is, the intellect is named as the capacity to throw away the husk and take the kernel.**"*

**And at 13/19-24 in `013-rad.md` the bearers of this attribute were tabulated in a list** (keeping the covenant, joining what should be joined, patience, spending in secret and in the open, repelling evil with good). **I rely on that treatment.**

**Here I add what belongs to Āl ʿImrān alone, and it is a fact verifiable within the sūra:** **the construct *ūlu'l-albāb* occurs twice in the sūra — at 7 and at 190.** And the two occurrences stand at the two ends of the sūra.

| Verse | Context | What they do |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | **Before the text** | Meeting the *mutashābih*, they are not thrown |
| **190-191** | **Before the universe** | They reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth |

**Two fields: the written text and the visible order. The same attribute.** **I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the sūra works the same capacity on two different objects of reading and names both of them "throwing away the husk and taking the kernel".

### What this says to the present

**What this verse says to the present goes beyond the bounds of a religious text, and I give this as an observation:**

When any text is read — a law, a contract, a scientific paper, a news report — there always stand before the reader two kinds of statement: **those that bear one sense** and **those open to more than one.** And the easiest way to misuse a text is always the same: **to leave aside what is plain and to push forward what is not.**

The verse describes this operation and catches it at two separate points:

| The verse's note | What it says |
|---|---|
| *Fī qulūbihim zaygh* | The problem is not in the text but in the **turning** |
| *Yattabiʿūna mā tashābaha* | **Selective reading** — tearing the part from the whole |
| *Ibtighāʾa'l-fitna* | The **aim**: not to understand but to confuse |
| *Hunna ummu'l-kitāb* | The **measure**: what is closed is returned to what is open |

**And the fourth row is a discipline of reading all by itself.** The most closed sentence of a text is read in the light of that text's most open sentences. If the reverse is done — if what is open is bent to fit what is closed — the text is no longer speaking; the reader is.

**The verse's choosing the word *zaygh* is exactly right for this reason:** an inclination is not a break. No one turns a text upside down all at once. **It begins with a small angle.**

---

## 3/8-9 — رَبَّنَا لَا تُزِغْ قُلُوبَنَا بَعْدَ إِذْ هَدَيْتَنَا وَهَبْ لَنَا مِن لَّدُنكَ رَحْمَةً إِنَّكَ أَنتَ ٱلْوَهَّابُ

*Rabbanā lā tuzigh qulūbanā baʿda idh hadaytanā wa hab lanā min ladunka raḥmah, innaka anta'l-wahhāb · Rabbanā innaka jāmiʿu'n-nāsi li-yawmin lā rayba fīh, inna'llāha lā yukhlifu'l-mīʿād*

"**Our Lord! Do not make our hearts swerve after You have guided us**, and grant us a mercy from Your presence. Truly You are **the great Bestower**. · Our Lord! You will **gather mankind for a day in which there is no doubt.** Truly God does not fail His appointment."

### The place of the prayer

**These two verses are the direct continuation of the one before, and the link is in the word: *lā tuzigh* — the same root (ز-ي-غ), in Form IV.**

| Verse | Mood | In whose mouth |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *fī qulūbihim **zaygh*** — a statement | **The text's** own note, about a third person |
| **8** | *lā **tuzigh** qulūbanā* — a prayer | **In the believer's** mouth, about himself |

**This is a link verifiable within the sūra, and it has to be recorded.**

**I give this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the seventh verse had described a defect in someone else; the eighth takes it as **a possibility for oneself**. **That is, immediately after the diagnosis the text turns the diagnosis back on the reader.** The first thing a person who learns of a defect would do is look for it in others; the verse closes that road.

**بَعْدَ إِذْ هَدَيْتَنَا** — "**after** You have given us guidance".

**The caveat itself matters:** the prayer consists not in asking for guidance but in asking that **what has been given be kept**. **And this agrees exactly with the picture of the root *zaygh*:** an inclination is what happens not to one who was never on the road but to **one who is on it**. The sun cannot decline from its zenith before it has reached it.

**ٱلْوَهَّاب** — root **و-ه-ب**: **to give without return.** *Wahhāb* is the intensive form *faʿʿāl*. **The distinguishing side of the root must be recorded:** a *hiba* is a giving not tied to any return or to any deserving. **And this agrees with the subject of the prayer:** the heart's staying straight is not asked for as something held on to by desert.

**And the word will occur once more later in the sūra, in the mouth of Zechariah:** *hab lī min ladunka dhurriyyatan ṭayyiba* (38) — **the same verb (*hab*), the same construction (*min ladunk*).**

| Verse | Who | What is asked |
|---|---|---|
| **8** | The believers | *Raḥmatan* — **a mercy** |
| **38** | Zechariah | *Dhurriyyatan ṭayyibatan* — **a goodly offspring** |

**Two prayers built with the same two words: *hab* and *min ladunk*.** This is a repetition verifiable within the sūra.

**لَا يُخْلِفُ ٱلْمِيعَادَ** — *mīʿād*, from the root **و-ع-د**: **the time or the place appointed.** **The root was treated at 13/31 in `013-rad.md` and at 39/20 in `039-zumer.md`.**

**And the word order of the sentence must be recorded:** the prayer begins with *rabbanā* (second person), but the last sentence shifts to the third person: ***inna'llāha* lā yukhlifu'l-mīʿād** — "truly **God** does not fail His appointment."

**This is the device called *iltifāt* in Arabic (a shift in the direction of address).** **I record this as an observation about the word order:** the sentence comes out of prayer and turns into a **ruling**. That is, the utterance is taken out of the mouth of the one praying and tied to a general note.

---

## 3/10-13 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ لَن تُغْنِىَ عَنْهُمْ أَمْوَٰلُهُمْ وَلَآ أَوْلَٰدُهُم مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ شَيْـًٔا … قَدْ كَانَ لَكُمْ ءَايَةٌ فِى فِئَتَيْنِ ٱلْتَقَتَا

*Inna'lladhīna kafarū lan tughniya ʿanhum amwāluhum wa lā awlāduhum mina'llāhi shayʾā, wa ulāʾika hum waqūdu'n-nār · Ka-daʾbi āli Firʿawna wa'lladhīna min qablihim, kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā fa-akhadhahumu'llāhu bi-dhunūbihim, wa'llāhu shadīdu'l-ʿiqāb · Qul li'lladhīna kafarū sa-tughlabūna wa tuḥsharūna ilā jahannama wa biʾsa'l-mihād · Qad kāna lakum āyatun fī fiʾatayni'ltaqatā, fiʾatun tuqātilu fī sabīli'llāhi wa ukhrā kāfiratun yarawnahum mithlayhim raʾya'l-ʿayn, wa'llāhu yuʾayyidu bi-naṣrihī man yashāʾ, inna fī dhālika la-ʿibratan li-ūli'l-abṣār*

"For those who disbelieve, neither their wealth nor their children will avail them anything against God… **like the way of the family of Pharaoh and of those before them**… Say to those who disbelieve: **you will be overcome**… **In two companies that met there was a sign for you:** one was fighting in the way of God, the other was disbelieving; **with the sight of the eye they saw them as twice their own number.** God supports with His help whom He wills. **In this there is surely a lesson for those who have eyes to see.**"

### دَأْب — root: د-أ-ب

**The concrete sense of the root is worth recording:** *daʾaba fī ʿamalih* — **to keep at a piece of work in the same way, without tiring.** *Daʾb* — a continuing habit, an accustomed course.

**That is, the verse does not say "like the condition of the family of Pharaoh"; it says "like their *course*".** **The word conveys not a single event but a repeated pattern.**

**I record this as an observation:** the choice of the word prepares in advance the line the sūra will lay down later in the Uḥud block. There too what will be described is not a single event but **a recurring law**: *qad khalat min qablikum sunan* (137).

### فِئَتَيْنِ ٱلْتَقَتَا — two companies

**The thirteenth verse names an encounter that took place in the past.** In the classical sources this is identified as **Badr**.

**Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I record this: the verse gives neither a place name nor a personal name.** The only thing it gives is **the attribute of the two sides**: one *tuqātilu fī sabīli'llāh*, the other *kāfira*. **In this commentary I do not go into the detail of the reports.**

### يَرَوْنَهُم مِّثْلَيْهِمْ رَأْىَ ٱلْعَيْنِ — a question of seeing

**The sentence is one of the most debated places in the classical commentaries, because the reference of the pronouns is undetermined.**

| Reading | Who sees whom | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Those who disbelieve**, the believers | The smaller side appears **twice its number** to the other side — fear falls on them |
| 2 | **The believers**, the other side | The larger side appears **twice its number** to the eye — and they stand firm even so |
| 3 | **The believers**, themselves | They see their own number as double and find confidence in it |

**All three readings are transmitted in the classical sources. I do not impose a preference.**

**And the note *raʾya'l-ʿayn* must be dwelt on: "with the sight of the eye".** That is, what is in question is not a guess or a supposition; it is recorded as something **seen with the eye**.

**I give this as an observation, and it holds whichever reading is taken:** the verse takes as its subject the **perceived** amount of the number, not the real amount. **And that what is decisive in a battle is often not the real number but how the two sides see each other is a thing observed throughout history.** I record this as a point of view; I am not dressing the verse in a modern thesis.

**لِّأُو۟لِى ٱلْأَبْصَٰرِ** — "for **those who have sight**." **And this belongs to the same family as the *ūlu'l-albāb* of the seventh verse.** In three separate places the sūra uses three separate constructions with "those who have": *ūlu'l-albāb* (7, 190), *ūlu'l-abṣār* (13), *ūlu'l-ʿilm* (18). **This is a verifiable fact.**

---

## 3/14-17 — زُيِّنَ لِلنَّاسِ حُبُّ ٱلشَّهَوَٰتِ مِنَ ٱلنِّسَآءِ وَٱلْبَنِينَ وَٱلْقَنَٰطِيرِ ٱلْمُقَنطَرَةِ مِنَ ٱلذَّهَبِ وَٱلْفِضَّةِ وَٱلْخَيْلِ ٱلْمُسَوَّمَةِ وَٱلْأَنْعَٰمِ وَٱلْحَرْثِ

*Zuyyina li'n-nāsi ḥubbu'sh-shahawāti mina'n-nisāʾi wa'l-banīna wa'l-qanāṭīri'l-muqanṭarati mina'dh-dhahabi wa'l-fiḍḍati wa'l-khayli'l-musawwamati wa'l-anʿāmi wa'l-ḥarth, dhālika matāʿu'l-ḥayāti'd-dunyā, wa'llāhu ʿindahū ḥusnu'l-maʾāb*

"**The love of the things desired has been made fair-seeming to people:** women, sons, hoarded heaps of gold and silver, branded horses, cattle and tilled land. **These are the provision of the life of this world; with God is the good place of return.**"

### زُيِّنَ — a passive verb

**The verb is passive: "was made fair-seeming."** The agent is not named.

**I record this as an observation about the word order, and the classical commentaries have dwelt on it:** the sentence does not name the one who adorns. Some exegetes read this as **a natural disposition**, others as **the work of Satan**. **Both are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.**

**And the object of the sentence must be recorded:** what is made fair-seeming is not the objects listed but ***ḥubbu'sh-shahawāt* — the *love* of them.** There are two steps: the object, and the love felt for the object. **The verse takes the second as its subject.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the construction itself:** the verse disparages none of the things listed — child, wealth, animal, tilled land. **The next sentence confirms this: *dhālika matāʿu'l-ḥayāti'd-dunyā* — "these are the provision of the life of this world."** *Matāʿ* is not a bad word; it means **what is used, what is drawn benefit from**. **What is at issue is not the existence of these things but the kind of bond between them and a person.**

### ٱلْقَنَٰطِيرِ ٱلْمُقَنطَرَة

The root **ق-ن-ط-ر**. *Qinṭār* — a large quantity; the lexicographers transmit different measures and **do not agree on any definite amount.** I give no figure in this commentary.

**And the construction is worth recording: *al-qanāṭīru'l-muqanṭara* — a noun and an adjective from the same root.** In Arabic this is called **emphasis**: "heaps heaped up".

**The word will occur once more in the sūra, at verse 75** — there as a measure of trust: *man in taʾmanhu bi-**qinṭārin** yuʾaddihī ilayk*. **This is a repetition verifiable within the sūra, and the work of the two occurrences differs:**

| Verse | What *qinṭār* does |
|---|---|
| **14** | **What is hoarded** — the love of which is made fair-seeming |
| **75** | **What is entrusted** — by which it is measured whether it is given back |

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the sūra names the same large quantity twice — once as possession, once as a trust. **And in the second occurrence what is measured is not the amount of the property but the conduct of the one holding it.**

### ٱلْخَيْلِ ٱلْمُسَوَّمَة

The root **س-و-م**: **to mark; and to turn loose (to pasture).** Both senses are transmitted:

| Reading | What *musawwama* is |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Marked** — branded, stamped; visibly of value |
| 2 | **Turned loose** — left out to pasture, free |

**Both are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.**

### وَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ — and verses 15-17

**The fifteenth verse sets a list against the list** (*jannātun tajrī min taḥtiha'l-anhār*), and the seventeenth verse counts **five attributes**:

| Attribute | Word |
|---|---|
| **The steadfast** | *al-ṣābirīn* |
| **The truthful** | *al-ṣādiqīn* |
| **The obedient** | *al-qānitīn* |
| **Those who spend** | *al-munfiqīn* |
| **Those who ask forgiveness before dawn** | *al-mustaghfirīna bi'l-asḥār* |

**The word order of the five must be recorded: all five are active participles** — that is, they convey not an **act** but a **continuance**. In Arabic the active participle conveys the one who is doing the work and the one characterised by that work.

**بِٱلْأَسْحَارِ** — root **س-ح-ر**: *saḥar*, the last part of the night. **The word was treated at 54/34 in `054-kamer.md` (*najjaynāhum bi-saḥar*).**

**I record this as an observation:** four of the five attributes are without a time; to the last of them **a time** is added. **And that time is the time when one is least seen.** The list closes with an act that has no visibility.

**And a comparison can be made with the list in the fourteenth verse — I give this as my own reading:** the first list counts **things possessed**, the second counts **things done**. In the first a person is the subject but there is no verb (*zuyyina* — passive); in the second there are five active participles. **That is, the sūra draws a distinction between "having" and "doing".**

---

## 3/18 — شَهِدَ ٱللَّهُ أَنَّهُۥ لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا هُوَ وَٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةُ وَأُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْعِلْمِ قَآئِمًۢا بِٱلْقِسْطِ

*Shahida'llāhu annahū lā ilāha illā huwa wa'l-malāʾikatu wa ūlu'l-ʿilmi qāʾiman bi'l-qisṭ, lā ilāha illā huwa'l-ʿazīzu'l-ḥakīm*

"**God has borne witness that there is no god but He** — and so have the angels and **those who possess knowledge**, **upholding justice.** There is no god but He; **al-ʿAzīz**, **al-Ḥakīm**."

### Three witnesses

**The word order of the verse must be recorded: a single verb (*shahida*), three subjects.**

| Witness | Who |
|---|---|
| 1 | **God** |
| 2 | **The angels** |
| 3 | ***Ūlu'l-ʿilm*** — those who possess knowledge |

**And the third place is worth recording:** the verse sets those who possess knowledge immediately after the angels. **This is a fact verifiable from the text, and the classical commentaries have dwelt on it a great deal.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** in the seventh verse came *rāsikhūna fi'l-ʿilm*; here *ūlu'l-ʿilm*. **Two constructions in the same sūra, eleven verses apart.** And both give knowledge not as a **position** but as a **stance**: the first is not thrown, the second bears witness.

### قَآئِمًۢا بِٱلْقِسْطِ — whose circumstance

**The word is in the accusative and is a circumstantial qualifier (*ḥāl*). But whose circumstance it is, is disputed:**

| Reading | Whose circumstance *qāʾiman* is | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **God's** | "Upholding justice" — it is He who bears witness, and He stands upholding justice |
| 2 | **All three witnesses'** | All three bear witness while upholding justice |
| 3 | **The witnessing itself's** | The witnessing is a witnessing borne upon justice |

**All three are transmitted. I do not impose a preference.**

**قِسْط** — root **ق-س-ط**. **The root has a curious feature, and it was recorded in the series at 55/9 in `055-rahman.md`:** in Form I (*qasaṭa*) the root means **to act unjustly**, in Form IV (*aqsaṭa*) **to act justly**. **That is, the same root turns to its opposite when the form changes.** *Qisṭ* — justice, the giving of a right in full.

---

## 3/19 — إِنَّ ٱلدِّينَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْإِسْلَٰمُ

*Inna'd-dīna ʿinda'llāhi'l-islām; wa ma'khtalafa'lladhīna ūtu'l-kitāba illā min baʿdi mā jāʾahumu'l-ʿilmu baghyan baynahum; wa man yakfur bi-āyāti'llāhi fa-inna'llāha sarīʿu'l-ḥisāb*

"**The religion with God is islām.** Those who were given the Book fell into division **only after knowledge had come to them**, out of **begrudging rivalry** among themselves. Whoever denies the signs of God — God is swift in reckoning."

---

**This verse and verse 85 are the two most-quoted and most misused verses of the sūra. Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I shall follow a procedure here:** first I shall give the root sense of the word, then recall how this word has been treated in the series, then record what the verse **does not say**.

### إِسْلَٰم — root: س-ل-م

**The concrete sense of the root was analysed in detail at 49/14 in `049-hucurat.md`, and I take the description given there:**

> *"**س-ل-م.** Its original sense is **to be sound, to be flawless, to be complete.** *Salāma* — soundness. *Salām* — peace, wholeness. *Silm* — peace. *Taslīm* — to hand a thing over complete. **At the centre of the root stands an *operation*: to hand something over to the other side in sound condition.** *Islām* is in Form IV: to hand oneself over."*

**I rely on that treatment.**

**And the two branches of the root must be recorded, because English covers them with two separate words:**

| Branch | Its English counterpart | Qurʾānic example |
|---|---|---|
| **Soundness / completeness** | **Peace, wholeness** | *Dāru's-salām*, *salāmun ʿalaykum* |
| **Handing over complete** | **Submission** | *Aslamtu li-rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* (al-Baqara 2/131) |

**The two branches are not separate:** to hand a thing over **complete** and to be **sound** come out of the same root. The one who hands over is the one who has kept nothing back.

### The distinction of *īmān* and *islām* — on the basis of 49/14

**`049-hucurat.md` 49/14 is the central text of this distinction**, and the table given there was this:

| | **إيمان** | **إسلام** |
|---|---|---|
| Picture of the root | The going away of fear, trusting | Handing a thing over complete |
| Where it happens | **In the heart** | **Outwardly** — in word and in conduct |
| Can it be seen | No | **Yes** |

**I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it here.**

**And a point recorded there carries over directly and settles how the verse is read:** in al-Ḥujurāt the refusal was read not as **an exclusion but as a correction of place** — *islām* is not a negative category but a positive word of the Qurʾān.

### What the verse says and what it does not say

**The wording of the verse is this: *inna'd-dīna ʿinda'llāhi'l-islām.* And the word order of the sentence must be recorded:**

- **Subject:** *al-dīn* — **definite** (with *al-*).
- **The caveat:** *ʿinda'llāh* — "**with God**".
- **Predicate:** *al-islām* — **definite.**

**In Arabic a nominal sentence whose two terms are both definite sets up an *identity*** — "A is B and consists of B." That is, the sentence is a sentence of definition.

**And the note *ʿinda'llāh* must not be passed over:** the sentence does not say "with people" or "in history". **It names the one who sets the measure.**

**Now, per the method (`USLUP.md`), I record this explicitly — these are limits that come out of the *wording* of the verse, not a softening brought in from outside:**

**One. The verse sets a *measure of acceptance*; it does not give a *list of persons*.** No community, people or group is named in the sentence. What is named is **the religion itself.**

**Two. The root sense of the word blocks any reduction of the verse to a historical-institutional name.** *Islām* is by its root **handing oneself over**, and the Qurʾān uses this verb of Abraham (*idh qāla lahū rabbuhū aslim qāla aslamtu li-rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*, al-Baqara 2/131), of the sons of Jacob (Yaʿqūb), of the disciples (3/52: *wa'shhad bi-annā muslimūn*) and of the queen who came to Solomon (Sulaymān). **These are facts verifiable from the text.**

**Three. The verse's own second sentence places on record how the first sentence is to be read.** The second sentence is this: *wa ma'khtalafa'lladhīna ūtu'l-kitāba **illā min baʿdi mā jāʾahumu'l-ʿilmu baghyan baynahum.*** **That is, the verse sets down the cause of the division as not ignorance but *baghy*.**

**Four. This commentary does not enter the debate about excommunication.** Who is inside and who is outside this definition is a ruling the verse itself does not make, and it will not be made here. **What the verse gives is a measure; applying the measure to persons is not the work of this commentary.**

### مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا جَآءَهُمُ ٱلْعِلْمُ بَغْيًۢا بَيْنَهُمْ

**This construction has been treated many times in the series, and at 45/17 in `045-casiye.md` a table of three places was drawn up:**

| Place | Expression |
|---|---|
| al-Baqara 2/213 | *Min baʿdi mā jāʾathumu'l-**bayyināt*** — *baghyan baynahum* |
| al-Baqara 2/253 | *Min baʿdi mā jāʾathumu'l-**bayyināt*** |
| al-Jāthiya 45/17 | *Min baʿdi mā jāʾahumu'l-**ʿilm*** — *baghyan baynahum* |
| al-Shūrā 42/14 | *Min baʿdi mā jāʾahumu'l-**ʿilm*** — *baghyan baynahum* |
| **Āl ʿImrān 3/19** | *Min baʿdi mā jāʾahumu'l-**ʿilm*** — *baghyan baynahum* |

**The same structure in five places. And in all of them the same thing is said: disagreement arises not from the *absence* of the evidence but *after* it has come.** **I rely on `002-bakara.md` 2/213, `042-sura.md` 42/14 and `045-casiye.md` 45/17 and do not repeat them here.**

**Here I add what belongs to Āl ʿImrān alone, and it is an observation about position:** in the other four places this sentence is **an independent** statement. **Here it is set immediately after a sentence of definition (*inna'd-dīna ʿinda'llāhi'l-islām*).**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the word order heads off a misuse by naming the cause of division immediately after the definition. As soon as the definition is given, it is recorded that the cause of splitting around that definition is **not knowledge but *baghy***. **That is, the verse meets, within the same verse, the turning of its own sentence into an instrument of contention.**

---

## 3/20-25 — فَإِنْ حَآجُّوكَ فَقُلْ أَسْلَمْتُ وَجْهِىَ لِلَّهِ وَمَنِ ٱتَّبَعَنِ … أَلَمْ تَرَ إِلَى ٱلَّذِينَ أُوتُوا۟ نَصِيبًا مِّنَ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ

*Fa-in ḥājjūka fa-qul aslamtu wajhiya li'llāhi wa mani'ttabaʿan, wa qul li'lladhīna ūtu'l-kitāba wa'l-ummiyyīna a-aslamtum, fa-in aslamū fa-qadi'htadaw, wa in tawallaw fa-innamā ʿalayka'l-balāgh, wa'llāhu baṣīrun bi'l-ʿibād*

"**If they dispute with you, say: I have handed my face over to God, and so have those who follow me.** Say to those who were given the Book and to **the *ummiyyūn*:** '**Have you handed yourselves over too?**' If they hand themselves over they have found the way; if they turn away, **your part is only to convey.**"

### أَسْلَمْتُ وَجْهِىَ لِلَّهِ

**The noun of the preceding verse (*al-islām*) comes here as a verb: *aslamtu*.**

**And its object must be recorded: *wajhiya* — "my face".** The root **و-ج-ه**: direction, face, the side of a thing towards which one turns. **The root was treated at 2/112 and 2/115 in `002-bakara.md`.**

**In Arabic *wajh* is used for the *whole* of a thing** — because the face is the side of a person that stands foremost and is recognised. **And the idiom occurs in the same form at al-Baqara 2/112:** *balā man aslama wajhahū li'llāhi wa huwa muḥsin*.

**I record this as an observation:** the nineteenth verse had named the religion; the twentieth turns that name into a **verb** and puts the verb in the **first person**. **That is, the definition is first shown on the speaker himself; then it is asked.**

**And the form of the question must be recorded: *a-aslamtum* — a question.** Not a command, not an imposition; **a question.** And its answer is met with two possibilities, the second of which sets the limit of conveying: *fa-innamā ʿalayka'l-balāgh.*

**ٱلْبَلَٰغ** — **a table of the limit of responsibility was drawn up in `047-muhammed.md`, at 42/48 in `042-sura.md`, at 46/35 in `046-ahkaf.md` and at 28/56 in `028-kasas.md`.** I rely on that treatment. **In summary: *balāgh* is to convey — not to make accepted.**

**ٱلْأُمِّيِّۦنَ** — from the root **أ-م-م**: those who are not people of a book. **The word was treated at 62/2 in `062-cuma.md`.** **Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I record this: the word here is not a disparagement but a term of *distinction*** — those who have not been given a book.

### Verses 21-25

**The twenty-first verse makes a severe statement:** *inna'lladhīna yakfurūna bi-āyāti'llāhi wa yaqtulūna'n-nabiyyīna bi-ghayri ḥaqqin wa yaqtulūna'lladhīna yaʾmurūna bi'l-qisṭi mina'n-nās* — "those who deny the signs of God, who **kill the prophets without right**, and who **kill those among the people who enjoin justice**…"

**Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I record this:** the sentence is built with ***alladhīna*** — that is, it is tied to an **attribute**, not to a community. No group is named in the sentence. **Whoever does these three things, the verse speaks of him.**

**And the third verb is worth recording, and it is usually passed over:** *wa yaqtulūna'lladhīna **yaʾmurūna bi'l-qisṭi mina'n-nās*** — "those who kill **those among the people who enjoin justice**."

**I record this as an observation:** the verse puts non-prophets into the same sentence. **That is, it is not required that the one killed be a prophet; it is enough that he enjoined justice.** And this is the first link in the line of "enjoining what is right" that will be built in verses 104 and 110 of the sūra.

**حَبِطَتْ أَعْمَٰلُهُمْ** (22) — root **ح-ب-ط**. **The concrete sense of the root was treated in `047-muhammed.md`:** an animal's eating a harmful plant and **swelling**, and that swelling ending in death. **That is, *ḥabṭ* is a swelling that deflates** — full from outside, empty within. **I do not repeat it here.**

**The twenty-third verse** describes how, when those given a portion of the Book are called to **the arbitration of their own book**, a group of them turns away: *thumma yatawallā farīqun minhum wa hum muʿriḍūn.*

**Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I record this, and it is a rule of reading that will be kept throughout the sūra:** the verse says ***farīqun minhum*** — "**a group** of them". **There is no blanket ruling.** **This note is directly connected with verse 113 of the sūra (*laysū sawāʾ*), and it will be shown there in a table.**

**The twenty-fourth verse gives the cause:** *dhālika bi-annahum qālū lan tamassana'n-nāru illā ayyāman maʿdūdāt* — "because they said, **the Fire will touch us only for a counted number of days.**"

**This sentence occurred word for word at 2/80 in `002-bakara.md` and was treated there.** I rely on that treatment. **In summary: the claim reduces not the *existence* of the punishment but its *duration*** — and this is a more insidious operation than a denial, because it goes on believing while rendering the outcome ineffective.

**غَرَّهُمْ فِى دِينِهِم مَّا كَانُوا۟ يَفْتَرُونَ** — root **غ-ر-ر**: to deceive; the concrete sense of the root is **a thing's outer face being bright and its inside empty**. **The root was treated at 31/33 in `031-lokman.md` and at 57/14 in `057-hadid.md`.**

**And the word order of the sentence must be recorded: the thing that deceives is not an enemy coming from outside — *mā kānū yaftarūn*, "what they themselves invented".** **That is, the verse ties the source of the deception to the one deceived.**

---

## 3/26-27 — قُلِ ٱللَّهُمَّ مَٰلِكَ ٱلْمُلْكِ تُؤْتِى ٱلْمُلْكَ مَن تَشَآءُ وَتَنزِعُ ٱلْمُلْكَ مِمَّن تَشَآءُ

*Quli'llāhumma mālika'l-mulki tuʾti'l-mulka man tashāʾu wa tanziʿu'l-mulka mimman tashāʾ, wa tuʿizzu man tashāʾu wa tudhillu man tashāʾ, bi-yadika'l-khayr, innaka ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr · Tūliju'l-layla fi'n-nahāri wa tūliju'n-nahāra fi'l-layl, wa tukhriju'l-ḥayya mina'l-mayyiti wa tukhriju'l-mayyita mina'l-ḥayy, wa tarzuqu man tashāʾu bi-ghayri ḥisāb*

"Say: '**O God, Owner of all dominion!** You give dominion to whom You will, **and You pull dominion away from whom You will.** You raise whom You will and You abase whom You will. **In Your hand is the good.** Truly You have power over all things. · **You make the night pass into the day, and You make the day pass into the night.** You bring the living out of the dead and the dead out of the living. And You provide for whom You will **without reckoning**.'"

### ٱللَّهُمَّ — a vocative form

**The word is a special vocative form standing in place of *yā Allāh*:** the *yā* drops and a doubled *mīm* comes at the end. **This form occurs in five places in the Qurʾān, and all five are sentences of calling or of entreaty.**

**I record this as an observation about the language;** the lexicographers differ over how the form is constructed, and I impose no preference.

### مَٰلِكَ ٱلْمُلْكِ — and four *mulk*s in a single verse

**The root م-ل-ك occurs four times, and each of the four is at a different piece of work:**

| Order | Word | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Mālika'l-**mulk*** | **The vocative** — the attribute of the one called |
| 2 | *Tuʾti'l-**mulka*** | The thing **given** |
| 3 | *Tanziʿu'l-**mulka*** | The thing **taken** |
| 4 | (*mimman tashāʾ*) | **From whom** the taken thing is taken |

**And the pairs of verbs must be recorded:**

| Giving | Taking |
|---|---|
| *Tuʾtī* — You give | ***Tanziʿu*** — You pull away |
| *Tuʿizzu* — You raise | *Tudhillu* — You abase |

The root **ن-ز-ع**: **to pull a thing out of its place, to wrench it loose.** **The choice of the root is worth recording:** the verse does not say "You take back" (*taʾkhudhu*). ***Nazʿ* is a pull that meets resistance** — as though the thing taken were holding on.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the difference between the two verbs:** for giving, *ītāʾ* (holding out and handing over) is used; for taking, *nazʿ*. **That is, the verse sets up an asymmetry between the way dominion is given and the way it is taken:** silent in the giving, contested in the taking.

### بِيَدِكَ ٱلْخَيْرُ — and a note on transcendence

**Per the method (`USLUP.md`), and following the note kept consistently throughout the series, the same holds here:** expressions such as *yad* (hand) cannot be understood as **ascribing an organ or a body** to God. **The same note was entered at 57/29 in `057-hadid.md` and in `036-yasin.md`:** two attitudes are transmitted in the classical commentaries — to accept the wording as it stands and leave its manner to God (*tafwīḍ*), or to interpret it in the sense of power and disposal (*taʾwīl*). **I transmit both and impose no preference.** The shared principle is `042-sura.md` 42/11.

**And there is something in the content of the sentence that must be recorded, and the classical commentaries have dwelt on it:** the sentence says *bi-yadika'l-**khayr*** — **only the good is named.** Although raising and abasing are counted together, it does not say "in Your hand are good and evil".

**The classical explanations:**

| # | Explanation |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Decorum** — naming only the good in address is what the language of prayer requires |
| 2 | **Coverage** — what is mentioned includes the other as well; in Arabic naming one of two opposites is common |
| 3 | **Direction** — since abasement too is tied to a wisdom, it is in the end inside the good |

**All three are transmitted. I impose no preference and do not enter the theological debate.**

### تُولِجُ ٱلَّيْلَ فِى ٱلنَّهَارِ — *īlāj*

The root **و-ل-ج**. **It was analysed in detail at 57/6 in `057-hadid.md` and tabulated at 31/29 in `031-lokman.md`.** I take the description given there:

> *"**Root: و-ل-ج.** Its concrete sense: **to enter a narrow place, to slip in, to seep inside.** […] The word itself describes a seeping. Night does not take the place of day; **it enters into it.**"*

**I rely on that treatment and do not repeat it here.**

**Here I add what belongs to Āl ʿImrān alone, and it is a difference of context:**

| Place | What *īlāj* occurs beside |
|---|---|
| **al-Ḥadīd 57/6** | Creation and **the knowledge of all things** (*ʿalīmun bi-dhāti'ṣ-ṣudūr*) |
| **Luqmān 31/29** | The **subjecting** of the sun and the moon |
| **Āl ʿImrān 3/27** | **The changing of hands of dominion** and **raising and abasing** |

**And the difference must be recorded — I give this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** in the other two places *īlāj* is a **proof of power**. **Here it does the work of a *comparison*.** The twenty-sixth verse had described the changing of hands of dominion; the twenty-seventh gives a picture that shows how this happens.

**For this is exactly the picture of *īlāj*:** night does not cut day off at a stroke; **the day grows a little shorter each day, then a little longer.** The boundary shifts every day, and on no day can it be said "there, today it changed." **And the changing of hands of dominion is most often like this too** — what appears as an overturning in a moment is the last step of a long seeping.

**I give this as a reading; the verse does not draw this comparison explicitly.** What it does draw is the standing of two sentences side by side, and that can be verified from the text.

### وَتُخْرِجُ ٱلْحَىَّ مِنَ ٱلْمَيِّتِ

**This sentence occurs in several places in the Qurʾān** (Yūnus 10/31, al-Anʿām 6/95, al-Rūm 30/19) **and was treated at 30/19 in `030-rum.md`.** I rely on that treatment.

**In the classical commentaries it is explained in two ways, and both are transmitted:**

| Reading | Its sense |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Material** — a plant from a seed, a living creature from an egg; life from dead earth |
| 2 | **Spiritual** — a believer coming out of an unbeliever, an unbeliever out of a believer |

**Both are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.** **And the second reading agrees with the context of the sūra:** from the thirty-third verse the sūra will relate **a lineage**, and within that lineage there will be both those who are chosen and others.

**بِغَيْرِ حِسَابٍ** — "without reckoning". **Two senses are given in the classical commentaries:** either "unlimited, immeasurably abundant", or "without being calculated according to the person's deserving". **Both are transmitted.**

**And the work the two verses do together can be recorded as my own reading:** the twenty-sixth verse describes a changing of hands **among people**, the twenty-seventh a changing of hands **in nature**. **Both are given with the same sequence of verbs** (You give–You take / You make pass–You make pass / You bring out–You bring out). **That is, the changing of hands of political power is put into the same sentence structure as the changing of places of night and day.**

**And this is what the verse says to the present:** the prayer states that power is not lasting, not as a threat but as **a description of an order**. **The verse neither praises nor blames any government;** it names the subject of the verbs of giving and taking. **No present-day political conclusion is drawn from this in this commentary.**

---

## 3/28-30 — لَّا يَتَّخِذِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ أَوْلِيَآءَ مِن دُونِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ

*Lā yattakhidhi'l-muʾminūna'l-kāfirīna awliyāʾa min dūni'l-muʾminīn, wa man yafʿal dhālika fa-laysa mina'llāhi fī shayʾin illā an tattaqū minhum tuqāh, wa yuḥadhdhirukumu'llāhu nafsah, wa ila'llāhi'l-maṣīr*

"**Let the believers not take those who disbelieve as protectors in place of the believers.** Whoever does that has no relation left with God — **except that you guard yourselves against a danger from them.** God warns you of Himself; and to God is the return."

### The extent of the word وَلِىّ

The root **و-ل-ي**: **to be near, to be adjoining.** *Walī* — the one who is near, who takes charge of another's affair. **The root was treated across eight occurrences in `042-sura.md` and in detail in `060-mumtehine.md`.**

**Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I record this, and it comes out of the wording of the verse:**

**One. The word is not "friend".** *Walāya* (the bond of mutual protection) means **entrusting one's affair to another, declaring one's side.** **This distinction was set out plainly at 60/8 in `060-mumtehine.md`:** *"God does not forbid you to **deal kindly and justly** with those who have not fought you over religion and have not driven you from your homes."* **I rely on that treatment.**

**Two. The verse's caveat is *min dūni'l-muʾminīn*** — "**in place of the believers**". This caveat shows what the prohibition is aimed at: a situation of choosing.

**Three. The verse does not give a blanket rule of conduct about a group of human beings;** it takes up a question of **allegiance**. **No legal ruling is drawn from this in this commentary.**

### إِلَّآ أَن تَتَّقُوا۟ مِنْهُمْ تُقَىٰةً

**The exception must be recorded:** the verse sets an exception to its own ruling. *Tuqāt*, from the root **ت-ق-ي** — **guarding oneself, taking precaution.**

**This exception has been discussed at length in the classical legal literature.** **Per the method (`USLUP.md`) I do not enter that discussion and I issue no legal ruling.** What is to be recorded is **that the verse itself sets an exception** — that is, the ruling is not absolute but qualified.

**The twenty-ninth verse** says that the hidden and the open are the same (*in tukhfū mā fī ṣudūrikum aw tubdūhu yaʿlamhu'llāh*), and **the thirtieth** that everyone will find what he has done **brought present**: *yawma tajidu kullu nafsin mā ʿamilat min khayrin muḥḍarā.*

**مُّحْضَرًا** — root **ح-ض-ر**: to be present. **A passive participle: brought present.**

**I record this as an observation:** the verse does not say that the deed is **recounted** but that it is **brought**. **And this is the very picture treated at 18/49 in `018-kehf.md` (*wa wajadū mā ʿamilū ḥāḍirā*).** I rely on that treatment.

**And the closing sentence is worth recording: *wa yuḥadhdhirukumu'llāhu nafsah… wa'llāhu raʾūfun bi'l-ʿibād.*** **In the same context, both a warning and a tenderness.** **I give this as an observation about the word order:** the sūra closes a heavy sentence with the name *Raʾūf* — and this lies along the same line as the pattern recorded in `049-hucurat.md` of "leaving room after every heavy sentence".

---

## 3/31-32 — قُلْ إِن كُنتُمْ تُحِبُّونَ ٱللَّهَ فَٱتَّبِعُونِى يُحْبِبْكُمُ ٱللَّهُ وَيَغْفِرْ لَكُمْ ذُنُوبَكُمْ

*Qul in kuntum tuḥibbūna'llāha fa'ttabiʿūnī yuḥbibkumu'llāhu wa yaghfir lakum dhunūbakum, wa'llāhu ghafūrun raḥīm · Qul aṭīʿu'llāha wa'r-rasūl, fa-in tawallaw fa-inna'llāha lā yuḥibbu'l-kāfirīn*

"Say: '**If you love God, follow me, and God will love you** and forgive you your sins.' · Say: '**Obey God and the Messenger.**' If they turn away — God does not love those who disbelieve."

### The testing of love

**The structure of the sentence must be recorded, and the classical commentaries have dwelt on it:**

- **The condition:** *in kuntum tuḥibbūna'llāh* — "if you love God"
- **The apodosis (a command):** *fa'ttabiʿūnī* — "follow me"
- **The outcome:** *yuḥbibkumu'llāh* — "God will love you"

**That is, the order is this: a love claimed → an act → a love answering to it.**

**I record this as an observation about the word order:** the verse does not reject the claim of love; **it ties it to something measurable.** Love is an inward state and is not seen from outside; *ittibāʿ* is seen.

**And the direction of the verbs is worth recording:**

| Sentence | The one who loves | The one loved |
|---|---|---|
| *Tuḥibbūna'llāh* | **The human being** | God |
| *Yuḥbibkumu'llāh* | **God** | The human being |

**Two directions, one root (ح-ب-ب), in the same verse.** And between them stands a verb. **I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the verse takes love out of being a one-way feeling and turns it into a two-way relation — and the visible link of the relation is the verb in the middle.

**وَيَغْفِرْ لَكُمْ ذُنُوبَكُمْ** — and forgiveness is set **after** love, not before. **I give this as an observation about the word order.**

**The thirty-second verse** closes the block: *aṭīʿu'llāha wa'r-rasūl.* **And this construction will occur once more in the sūra, at verse 132 — inside the Uḥud block.**

| Verse | Context |
|---|---|
| **32** | At the end of the block of **the Book and the dispute** |
| **132** | Inside the block of **war** |

**The same command, on two separate grounds.** This is a repetition verifiable within the sūra. **And at verse 152 the opposite of the same root will come: *wa ʿaṣaytum* — "and you disobeyed".** The sūra sets down the command twice and records its breach once.

---

## 3/33-34 — إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ ٱصْطَفَىٰٓ ءَادَمَ وَنُوحًا وَءَالَ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ وَءَالَ عِمْرَٰنَ عَلَى ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · ذُرِّيَّةًۢ بَعْضُهَا مِنۢ بَعْضٍ

*Inna'llāha'ṣṭafā Ādama wa Nūḥan wa āla Ibrāhīma wa āla ʿImrāna ʿala'l-ʿālamīn · Dhurriyyatan baʿḍuhā min baʿḍ, wa'llāhu samīʿun ʿalīm*

"**God chose Adam (Ādam), Noah (Nūḥ), the family of Abraham (Ibrāhīm) and the family of ʿImrān above all beings** · **as one offspring, one from another.** God is hearing, knowing."

### ٱصْطَفَىٰ — root: ص-ف-و

**The concrete sense of the root is worth recording:** **ص-ف-و — a thing's being clear, pure, free of sediment.** *Ṣafwa* — the cleanest part of a thing; *ṣafī* — clear water.

**Form VIII (*iftiʿāl*): *iṣṭafā* — to pick out and take the *pure* part of a thing.** **That is, there is a *sifting* inside the verb:** choosing is taking the clear out of the mixed.

**And the distribution of the verb within this sūra must be recorded — this is a fact verifiable from the text:**

| Verse | Sentence | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **33** | *Inna'llāha **iṣṭafā** Ādama…* | **Four: Adam, Noah, the family of Abraham, the family of ʿImrān** |
| **42** | *Inna'llāha **iṣṭafāki** wa ṭahharaki wa **iṣṭafāki** ʿalā nisāʾi'l-ʿālamīn* | **Mary — twice** |
